## THE CAMPAIGN OF

#### 1812 IN RUSSIA by CARL VON CLAUSEWITZ

This campaign study was originally published in German in Carl von Clausewitz’s collected work: Hinterlassene Werke des Generals Carl von Clausewitz über Krieg und Kriegfiihrung, 10 vols. (Berlin: Dummlers Verlag, 1832–37), Vol. 7 (1835), which contained "Der Feldzug von 1812 in Russland"; "Der Feldzug von 1813 his zum Waffenstillstand"; and "Der Feldzug von 1814 in Frankreich." The version in your hands (or on your screen) is an English translation published (anonymously) by Francis Egerton, Lord Ellesmere, through John Murray Publishers, London, in 1843. Egerton's translation has been reprinted frequently, e.g., Stackpole Books, ISBN 1853671142; Academic International, 1970; with an introduction by George Nafziger, London: Greenhill, 1992; with a Foreword by Gerard Chaliand,1997, ISBN 0962871583; with an introduction by Sir Michael Howard, New York: Da Capo Press, 1995; etc. The errata identified by the publisher (on pp.40, 115) have not been corrected in this PDF.

This translation is the only full English rendition of Clausewitz's study of the Russian campaign, in which Clausewitz participated as a Russian staff officer because his native Prussia was occupied by the French. It is rather old in language and style, though quite readable. Readers seeking a better grasp of Clausewitz's thinking about the campaign should consult the modern and more scholarly—but only partial—translation in Carl von Clausewitz, Historical and Political Writings, eds./trans. Peter Paret and Daniel Moran (Princeton: Princeton University Press, 1992), pp.110–204. The findings of the 1812 study (though not its personal revelations) were woven into Clausewitz’s famous theoretical exploration of war, Vom Kriege (On War).

Clausewitz had been a participant in a number of the campaigns discussed in his collective works, especially those of 1806, 1812, 1813, 1814, and 1815. Clausewitz normally minimized or even completely ignored his own role and experiences in the campaign under discussion, but in the case of 1812 he discusses his own experiences in considerable detail. He thereby opens a useful window into his otherwise somewhat mysterious personality. That personality has been the subject of much mythmaking—most of which, it now clearly appears, has been wildly wrong.

A NOTE ON THE TRANSLATOR Francis Egerton (1800-1857) was born Lord Francis Leveson-Gower (his name until 1833) and became the 1st Earl of Ellesmere and Viscount Brackley in 1846. Egerton's interest in Clausewitz was motivated by his friendship with the first Duke of Wellington. Wellington's circle became very interested in Clausewitz's work, particularly his study of the campaign of 1815. Egerton's discussions with Wellington concerning Clausewitz's Der Feldzug von 1812 in Russland are recounted in Personal

Reminiscences of the Duke of Wellington by Francis, the First Earl of Ellesmere, edited by his daughter Alice, Countess of Strafford (London: John Murray, 1904).

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

THE work here translated forms a portion only of the seventh volume of a posthumous series of military treatises, which have been edited by a sister of their deceased Author, and which, as she informs their readers, were not revised or prepared by him for the press. As the Author, though his long and active services raised him to posts of trust in the service of his country, never attained a situ ation of independent and conspicuous command in war, a slight sketch of his biography may be neces sary to enable English readers to form some esti mate of his qualifications for military criticism. Carl von Clausewitz was born in 1780, in the Prus sian village of Burg. He entered the military service of his country at the early age of twelve, and assisted at the campaigns of 1 793 and 1 794. He seems thenceforward to have applied himself to the scientific branches of his profession. In 1801 he entered the military school of Berlin, then con ducted by that eminent soldier Scharnhorst, by A 3

vi TRANSLATOR ' S PREFACE.

whom he was soon distinguished and patronised. He assisted at the disastrous campaign of 1806, in which he was wounded and taken prisoner. On his return from France he was placed on the staff of his patron Scharnhorst, and employed in the secret preparations for the renovation of the military resources ofPrussia, to which that officer assiduously devoted himself. He was also selected as military instructor to the Crown Prince, now the reigning sovereign. He relates in the present work the course he pursue!f on the occasion of the rupture between France and Russia, from which it wil l appear that he made the famous campaign which he describes in the Russian service, and in various capacities. After its conclusion he remained in the service of Russia, but attached to Blucher's corps, till the period of the armistice which interrupted the campaign of 1 813. He was afterwards trans ferred, as chief of the staff, to the corps of General W allmoden, who commanded a Russo-German division of the army of the North under Bernadotte. His name is mentioned with distinction on several occasions, and particularly in the affair of Goehrde. In 1815 he re-entered the service of his country, and made the campaign of the Netherlands as quarter master-general to the corps of General Thielman. At the peace he was appointed by his sovereign,

S PREFACE. vi i

:first to a general's command on the Rhine, and next 
to· the direction of the military school of Berlin. 
This sketch may serve to show that the circum· 
stances of his career, and his opportunities for ob 
servation and study, were such as to entitle, prima 
facie, his professional works to a consideration 
which is, we believe, in his own country a high 
one. 
The Russian campaign, the greatest transaction 
of civilised warfare on record, has found several 
historians of merit among the eye-witnesses of its 
occurrences. Of their works, such as are written 
in the French language, and, with the exception 
perhaps of the Russian Buturlin, by officers in the 
service of Buonaparte, are best known to English 
readers. As a foreigner in Russia, General Clau 
sewitz was at least free from the national partialities 
which to some extent may have influenced the pen 
either of a French or Russian author. As an actor, 
he enjoyed every facility for information, except 
the important one, indeed, of a knowledge of the 
Russian language. We learn from the preface of 
his sister, the Editress, that no false delicacy on her 
part has induced her to suppress certain remarks 
on individuals, which he would perhaps have been 
reluctant to see published in his lifetime, and 
which, being preserved unaltered, make him, to a 
A 4 

viii TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

certain extent, the Clarendon of the crisis he de scribes. With reference to these various circum stances, we have thought that this translated sample of the works of an Author much respected on the Continent might prove acceptable both to military and other readers. We must, however, declare in limine, that the style of the Author is dry, and his treatment of his subject professional. Those who seek for those adornments of language, to which that subject so strongly invites an eyewitness of horrors which no language can ful ly describe, much less exaggerate, wil l do better to turn to the pages of Segur, La Baume, and others. The second division of the work is out of its place, and unconnected with the other two, being a mere detail of dates, movements, and numbers, af fording to a professional reader means of easy and we presume accurate reference, but destitute of the interest which, in the case of the other two, attaches to the Author's personal narrative. Of this, perhaps the most interesting portion is that which concerns the famous York convention. In the former part of the campaign, while the Author was serving with the main army, his ignorance of the Russian language made him little more than an intelligent spectator, embar rassed . rather than assisted by nominal functions.

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. ix

In this transaction he became an useful and trusted agent to General Diebitsch, himself a Prus sian ; and many of the details which he is thus enabled to give of the conduct and bearing of the Monk of Prussia are, we apprehend, new to the world. The situation of York, as described by the Author, though without any attempt to quit his usual dry and matter-of-fact style, is essentially dramatic, and the al lusion of the Author to the Wal lenstein of Schiller's tragedy is just and appro priate. We know of no situation in history which renders it more difficult for the reader of after times to pass a verdict clear and satisfactory to himself on the principal actor. The prejudices and partialities of the time, national and political, might easily have hurried men to a decision, and made of York a traitor or an hero; but when these have passed away the question becomes nicer. Our own opinion is, that the action cannot be fully justified, and yet we give that opinion somewhat in the spirit of the Irish counsel, who, while dilating, as his brief required, on the enormity committed by a party to a duel, was suddenly asked by the judge how he would have himself acted towards his client under the circumstances, and replied, " By the living G-d, my Lord, I would have dilacerated him upon the spot." Without endeavouring further to adjust

X TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

the balance between impeachment and apology, we content ourselves with the quotation of Thomson's well-known line applied by him to a case of nearly equal delicacy-

" Say, ye severest, what would you have done ? "

The Author's account of the af fair of the Beresina is instructive as to the movements of the dif ferent Russian corps, and the causes of their failure; which, as he was then serving with Wittgenstein's army, he was favourably situated for observing. Tschitschagow has usually borne the blame of Buonaparte's escape on that occasion, and he expi ated the mistakes he unquestionably committed by years of exile and disgrace. From the Author's account we gather, that Wittgenstein was not totally blameless. It is difficult, however, to avoid the conclusion, that Kutusow was the most culpable of the three, unless the two positive statements of the Author be disputed-first, that the Admiral re ceived from Kutusow himself the erroneous state ment of Buonaparte's direction, which led to his own false movement to the south; and next, that, in spite of all previous mistakes, Kutusow had time to have brought up his own troops at the critical place and hour. It is pretty clear, that al l the Russian generals hesitated positively to drive the

TRANSLATOR'S !'REFACE. xi hunted animal to bay. It has been said of Buona parte in his great campaigns, that his presence on any point was usually reckoned, by military calcu lators on either side, at the value of 40,000 .men. Its moral value, perhaps, never stood him in better stead than at this most critical and desperate pas sage of his fortunes-the bridge of Studianka. We almost doubt whether the great qualities either of the French army or its chief were so conspicuous in the conception and execution of their most bril liant victories, as in this, the last scene of the Rus sian tragedy. The reader wil l find at p. 158. some remarks on a particular passage of the battle of Borodino, which, we apprehend, will be found to be at variance with the views of many other authorities, and es pecial ly with those of Russians in general. We al lude to his narrative of the cavalry attack under Ouwarow, made, or rather intended to be made, on the French left flank and rear during the heat of the action. This movement has usually been con sidered a deep-laid scheme of Kutusow, probably forming an important feature in his plan for the operations of the day. The Author positively states it to have been an accident, suggested at the mo ment by a chance observation of Platoff, and adopted without much inquiry or consideration by Kutusow.

xii TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

His description is rather graphic of the old Tartar, with his head confused by the rapid strokes of the hammer which the Thor of modern history was wielding about his ears, yielding at once a portion of a precious reserve to the solicitation of some young and ardent subordinates. Supposing the Author's personal observation to have guided him to the truth in this particular, we are yet inclined to doubt, whether he does not rather undervalue even the actual as well as the probable effect of the operation. There can be no question now, that it was undertaken on too smal l a scale for its purpose at so early a period of the battle; and, perhaps, as little can it be questioned, that the endeavour at its execution was feeble, even with reference to that scale. It would appear, however, from other ac counts, that, inadequate as it was in means, and feeble in their employment, it excited much alarm in the enemy's ranks, and caused hesitation and delay in the advance of Eugene. It is surely not beyond reasonable calculation to suppose, that a dashing partisan like Tchernicheff might, even without committing his horsemen to certain de struction, have improved an advantage, which even Ouwarow's over-caution could not altogether neu tralise. To kill the poys and the luggage is, we know, against the law of arms; but even Colonel

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. xi ii

Fluellen would have seen no impropriety in cutting up traces, horses, and riders, of reserve artil lery; and Buonaparte's own bul letin from Waterloo ac knowledges the effect of such an operation, as executed by General Ponsonby's brigade of cavalry, which reached the reserve batteries of D'Erlon's di vision, and effectual ly disorganised them; and this not by an unforeseen flank or rear attack, but by a fair rush at and through the front. Observing that our Author mentioned Italian troops as opposed to Ouwarow, we examined a history of the campaign by an officer of that nation, avowedly composed for the record and glorification of the gal lant actions and very gal lant we believe they were-of his coun trymen in Russia. We found in it, as we hoped, special mention of the incident of the battle in question; and as the writer, whether qualified and accurate or not in other respects, is a competent witness to the moral effect produced at the moment on himself and his companions in arms, we shal l quote some of his narrative. After describing the movement he proceeds :-"This movement not only afforded the hope of an useful and powerful diversion, calculated to liberate the centre which had been compromised, but flattered the Russians with the prospect of new and im mense results. Every unexpected incident in

XIV TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

war is an occasion for terror. It is certain that this unexpected irruption would have spread dis order and confusion in the rear of the French anny amid that immense accumulation of artil lery, bag gage, camp followers, wounded, &c., in the fields near the great road. These, flying to and collect ing on the ground where the army was struggling with an obstinate enemy, would have made it uncertain in its blows, and doubtful in its move ments; or else, wandering where chance led them, would have been an easy prey to the Russians. An army, distant 600 miles from its resources and reinforcements, or any point of support, might, without imputation of· timidity, show a momentary apprehension at such an unexpected aggression, which threatened to deprive it of every means of salvation and escape." The writer proceeds to describe in the style of a French bul letin, which contrasts strongly with the Teutonic simplicity of our Author, how the guard, having just obtained permission to march on the great redoubt which had hitherto foiled the efforts of Gerard and others, were just encountering, with great satisfaction to themselves, the fire of 100 pieces of artil lery, and replying with shouts of" Viva 1' Imperatore! " to the various agreeable noises of · such an orchestra, which he enumerates with much

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. XV particularity. He next relates the dismay produced by the reports of Ouwarow's appearance, and of the expected retirement of Delzons and Ornano, and the exposure of the Italian batteries, the baggage, and the villages of W oina and Borodino. He says further, " The enemy's movement was calculated with wisdom, for, in case of reverse, he had his retreat secured by the cross roads which terminate in the great Moscow road, the only one occupied by the French in their rear. If this movement had produced nothing more than the massacre of the unfortunates in the rear, the capture of the reserve park and ammunition, the diversion from its place of a corps of reserve, so preciously bus banded by the Emperor, the stagnation of the French movements, and the audacity infused into those of Russia, would have effected much. " We omit the details by which the Italian aggran dises into a splendid feat of arms the repulse of a comparatively smal l body of cavalry, which was of course foiled when it hesitated. The writer states, however, positively, that the guard was checked in its advance and

.

hurried back to the point threatened. He adds, " This diversion, though unsuccessful, was of advantage to Kutusow, as it had excited uneasi-·ness in the mind of Napoleon, who could not know the precise nature of the attack, or guess its

XVI THANSLATOH ' S PREFACE, results. It retarded the attack of the principal redoubt, a circumstance fatal, not only on account of loss of time, but because the Italian artil lery, which was to co-operate in its assault, began to be overmatched by the Russian pieces, which they had taken the opportunity of the delay to dispose to advantage in the works." • We infer altogether that the principle of the attack was sound, and that Platoff at least deserves more credit for its suggestion than the Author is willing to allow. In the Russian summer ma nreuvres of 1839, when the drama of Borodino was represented for a second time, but without French performers, Ouwarow's episode was repeated with great applause, but, as we understand, on a much more effective scale than on the first performance. t

"' Mr. Alison says in his history, founding his observations on Fain and Chambray, "This diversion had an important effect, and, by withdrawing a portion of the reserve destined for the attack of the great redoubt, sensibly retarded the success of the day." t These reviews took place in the year 1 839. The Emperor, we believe, had summoned to them all the officers yet surviving in his service, who had been engaged in the action twenty-seven years before. The field of Borodino had not undergone such alterations as have totally changed the face of that of Waterloo. Even the redoubts, we believe, still retain their shape. The. spectacle must have been one of deep interest to General Yer malof and others, who assisted at it.

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. xvn

Of al l the opinions, however, put forward by the Author, the one most at variance with the com monly received version of events, and we must say in every point of view the most paradoxical, is his theory as to the motive and object of Buonaparte's movement from Moscow towards Kaluga. • It has been usual ly imagined - we are not aware that the idea has ever before been controverted that Buonaparte intended by this movement to throw at least his main and fighting force on a new line of retreat, that which leads to Smolensko from Kaluga by Jelnia, one which would, as has been always al leged, have led him through a richer and unexhausted territory. His enormous train of wounded, baggage, &c., we presume in this case would have taken, under sufficient escort, the route to Smolensko by which the army had marched on Moscow. How this column might have fared at the handS of Winzingerode's corps from the north, it is not within our province to conjecture. It is said, indeed, that at an earlier period Buonaparte, with a view of easing the exhausted road to Smo lensko, had contemplated a line of retreat more to the north by Bjeloi, direct to Witebsk, leaving Smolensko to the south. t However this may

• Page 199. 

t It has also been stated that Eugene, to the last, wished to a

XVlll TRANSLATOR ' s PREFACE.

have been, it has hardly before been doubted that the movement towards Kaluga was adopted with an earnest intention to reach that place, and then to take the new line of retreat by J elnia. The belief has been equal ly general, that one of the great misfortunes of the campaign was the failure of this project ; and that, though Kutusow was defeated at Malo Jaroslawetz, that defeat was in its results a victory; because the resistance of t.he Rus sians was sufficient to compel Buonaparte to revert to the main road by which he had advanced. Ac cording to our Author, the reason and motive of Buonaparte's movement was merely this: Kutusow from the camp of Tarutino had three marches less to make to Smolensko than Buonaparte. It· was necessary, therefore, for Buonaparte to begin .his retreat by attacking and stunning him, unless he could manreuvre him to a sufficient distance. He says that with regard to the line of J elnia, it was impossible that Buonaparte should have contem plated it, for he had no magazines on that road, no time to profit by the supposed resources of that line of country, and would have been starved in a week. This reasoning may be very sound, and may con take this route with his corpe, but was refused by B uonaparte� He would in this case have avoided Smolensko,. and have en deavoured to join Victor or Oudinot against Wittgenstein.

this late date begins to doubt the possibility of the

ments for the subsistence of his troops, and might

We have thought it desirable to cal l the attention

the campaigns of such a man as Buonaparte is

more competent critical investigation than ours. 
The Author's opinion with regard to the effect of

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. XXI believe, that the reckoning of that event among the main causes of Buonaparte's discomfiture is one of those vulgar errors which often establish them· selves in popular belief, with respect to great transactions. It was doubtless an inconvenience, a disappointment ; but the Author shows, we think, good reason for his belief, that the preservation of the city and its contents could not have averted the total failure of the expedition; j that the military position of the French in Moscow was essentially false, and their force inadequate to maintain itself there for the winter. In short, the Russians had only to persevere in refusing to treat for peace; and the only questions which remained were, whether and how Buonaparte could extricate himself from his enterprise, without total ruin to his army. J By an earlier retreat, perhaps, he might have effected this. In this view it would almost appear that the conflagration might have turned to his profit. • It might at least have suggested both a reason and an excuse for that earlier retreat. We now know with tolerable certainty, that Buonaparte was amused by the prospect of a peace; that he lingered to the last in this delusive hope. It is probable,

• Since this was written, we .find the opinion of the French General, Mathieu Dumas, to the same effect, quoted by Mr. Alison, vol. viii. p. 713. 

a 3

xxii TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

that he relied much on the recollection and con sciousness of the personal influence he had obtained of old, at Tilsit, over the mind of Alexander. He must, however, have known enough of the world to have learned, though hardly as yet in his own person, that there is no such thing as an unmiti gated autocracy, and that though the autocratic principle has been perhaps carried as far in Russia as in any great community of the world, except China, in a great national struggle a Czar, like sovereigns of humbler title, must be influenced by popular feeling and opinion. The real cause of the conflagration is to this hour unknown. We know very well that Buonaparte himself could have and had no hand in it, but at the moment, and in Russia, it was natural ly attributed to him, and the consequent exasperation of the public mind among al l classes was an agent of all others most likely to counteract any influence of that captivation which Buonaparte's personal intercourse exercised on Alexander. As an excuse to Europe for a timely retreat, it would have been most profitable-it is none for the consequences of delay. The Author has left unnoticed another prevalent theory, which we are inclined on good authority also. to class as a 'vulgar error. The early frost has been to Buonaparte and his admirers, what the

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. xx:iii

great fire at Ravenswood Castle was to Caleb Bal 
derstone, who appealed to it ever after, to account 
for the deficiencies of housekeeping, and the absence 
of domestic furniture. It was in this spirit that 
Buonaparte sanctioned the admission of a medal 
into the numismatic series of his career, commemor 
ative of his disastrous retreat, on which an angel is 
represented, opening an 1Eolian bag of tempests on 
the French army. I It is, however; the opinion of 
competent judges acquainted with the country, that 
the early rains of a milder winter would have made 
the actual movement and progress of a retreating 
army still more difficult than it proved, and would 
perhaps have accelerated a catastrophe which cer 
tainly no cause or circumstance could materially 
have aggravated ; l for, short of Buonaparte's per 
sonal capture and that of his principal generals, it 
, was complete. 
In expressing, however, an opinion that the mere 
effect of temperature has been over-estimated by 
those who attribute principally to this cause the 
destruction which ensued, we mean to confine our 
selves to the French retreat previous to the passage 
of the Beresina, which took place on 29th N ovem 
ber. A partial thaw had increased the difficulty 
of that arduous operation, but on the 3d December 
the thermometer began rapidly to fall, and on the 
a 4 

XXIV TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

7th, the cold is said to have reached the terrible intensity of from 26 to 30 degrees below zero ; an extreme fatal to the frame of man when unprotected by means which an army on the retreat cannot command. This fell as heavily on the fresh troops which arrived from the various corps of reserve as on those which had made the exhausting march from Moscow. I The Marquis de Chambrai says, vol. ii i. p. 116., that Loison's division, which joined to form the rear-guard at O!l.:miana 10,000 strong, lost 7000 men by the cold in three days. Even here, however, the author just quoted considers that the cold was favourable to the escape of a remnant of the French army, and still more so to the prospects of Napoleon for the next campaign. It stopped the advance of Kutusow, who might otherwise have gleaned the last debris of the army, and have pushed forward at once into Germany. Perhaps since the siege of Jerusalem no more frightful mass of misery was ever accumulated within the wal ls of one city than that which the Emperor Alexander found fermenting in Wilna on his arrival. He was not a man likely to omit any measures for its alleviation, but it is due to his brother Constantine, who has left a less amiable reputation behind him, to observe that he en dangered his life by his exertions in carrying sue-

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. XXV cour into the infected dens in which the French prisoners were heaped. He caught in the principal prison, the Convent of St. Basil, the fever which was raging in the precincts of that charnel where frozen corpses were piled against . the windows tO exclude the numbing influence of the external air. An iron constitution saved him for the further exhibition of the eccentricities of a disposition too frequently displayed in acts of ferocity, but not destitute of some redeeming features. Altogether, the work we have endeavoured to translate bears the testimony of a competent and enlightened witness, to a fact of historical, scientific, and moral importance. That the Russian expedi tion of Buonaparte was too vast an undertaking for the greatest accumulation of military means ever collected, conducted and handled by as great a performer on such an instrument as possibly the world has ever known. If the lessons of history are worth any thing as warnings to ambition, re bukes to pride, and checks to the abuse of power, this lesson must have its value. Up to Smolensko, indeed, it may be considered that Buonaparte had a military chance of success ; but we think it clearly made out, that this chance was confined to the bare possibility of his inducing the Russians to meet him in unequal battle at the outset, a chance which xx:vi TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

depended not so much on his own positive skil l, as on the negative condition, of the total absence of skill and prudence in his adversary. This was evidently a part of his plan ; but it is hardly a jus tification of him, that the Russians were in fact more than once on the verge of playing this game into his hands. We surely have no right to reckon on the error of an enemy, when it is one which he can easily avoid committing if he chooses, and which it requires little short of judicial blindness in him to incur. The question was not whether he coUld win a great batt.le should he fight one. His superiority of force, coupled with the recollection of his former exploits, and the consciousness of his own genius, entitled him to risk almost any stake on the con tingency of such a conflict. The question was whether he could command the essential condition of fighting one at all� After Smolensko it was too late. Numbers began to verge 'towards equality, skill and genius to be balanced by local disadvan tages, and as to mere bravery, Eylau alone, or even his triumph at Friedland, might have taught him that sweeping victories were not to be expected from any moral deficiency in that quality on the part of the Russians. The advance to Moscow of 600 miles through the territory of a great military

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. :X Xvl l power, not reduced or - exhausted by recent disas trous campaigns, was an undertaking which no calculation could justify. Nothing but what we shortsighted mortals cal l chance, could have saved it from failure, and the utter failure it experienced was not due to such chance, either in the shape of the fire of Moscow, or the premature severity of winter, but was the natural and calculable conse quence of the misapplication of great means, and the misdirection of irresponsible power, to an unat tainable object. We cannot conclude these prefatory remarks without expressing an opinion that the Author has stopped short of justice in his observations on Bar clay. The Author's own testimony clears him of any share in the expensive strategical mistake of the Camp of Drissa. If he had fal len into the snare there prepared for him by the incapacity of Alex ander's advisers, he would have borne the blame which- he had not fairly earned. It is hardly just to deny him the ful l honour of his successful escape from a danger not of his own creation. From Wilna to Drissa he was in a false position, such as we might imagine that of the Duke of Wellington, if we could suppose the case of George Ill. at two days' distance on the 'line of his retreat to Torres V edras urging him to hurry his progress least xxvii i TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE.

Massena should be at Lisbon before him. ·Here the parallel would cease, for a worse imitation of Torres V edras than the Camp· of Drissa it would be difficult to conceive. An imitation it probably was, and one which gave Buonaparte a reasonable prospect of achieving a second J ena in Lithuania. " Could I," says he in his Memoirs, "expect that the Russians would abandon this camp which had cost them an year's labour and vast sums?" It can but seldom happen that the sovereign of a state should unite in his own person the genius and acquirements necessary for the conduct of an army in the :field. The history indeed of modern Europe presents three remarkable instances, in each of which an hereditary sovereign was obviously so endowed. Gustavus Adolphus, Charles XII., and Frederic the Great, constitute a trio, to which it would be difficult to add a fourth. The Emperor Alexander wil l assuredly never be cited as such, and though he was far from deficient either in talents or bravery, it is clear that, in the case of the Russian Campaign, his presence at head-quarters was detrimental and dangerous, and that his abilities and energies, considerable as they were, found far better employment at Petersburgh than at Wilna. History wil l record of Barclay that, after escaping from the mouse-trap of Drissa, holding a doubtful

TRANSLATOR'S PREFACE. XXIX and disputed authority, stigmatised as a foreigner, harrassed by intrigues, scarcely able to curb the desire for unequal battle in the ranks of a brave but uninstructed army, he led away his far inferior force safe from before the first rush of such an op ponent as Buonaparte, and handed it over with honourable resignation to a successor in command, untouched by serious discomfiture, and even en couraged by partial successes. Scotland has reason to be proud that blood of hers flowed in the veins of the son of the Livonian pastor.

EDITOR'S PREFACE.

THE Campaign of 1812, which figures in this volume, consists of three chapters, of which the second alone ,was originally intended for the Au thor's proposed work, the first and, third being apparently destined for another. The second con tains a review of the campaign, while the two others are devoted rather to a description of the events in which the Author was personally impli cated. These two are evidently connected together; while the second is an interpolation, and an inter ruption of the thread of the narrative. They are left, however, in the position in which they were found by the Editor, who has not thought herself justified in making any alterations . . As, alas ! among the papers of the Author no history of the Campaign of 1813 has been dis covered, it has been thought proper to print a xxxii EDITOR'S PREFACE.

sketch of its first period, which was written by 
particular desire • during the armistice of 1813. 
There is no doubt that the Author had reasons 
for omitting this Campaign from his aeries, but 
they are not explained. We must always lament, 
that the premature stroke which deprived his coun 
try and his friends of one so dear to them pre 
vented him also from completing his proposed 
task, and not alone from adding what is wanting, 
but from revising what is extant, as he had pur 
posed. Many a criticism, too harshly perhaps 
expressed in the first animation of feeling, would 
have been softened down, many a subsequent elu 
cidation have been turned to account, and the 
present sketch have been a finished work. As 
true connoisseurs, however, best detect from a 
sketch the hand of a master, it was hoped that the 
intrinsic merit of this work would be rightly esti 
mated in despite of its imperfect form, that can 
dour and sagacity would recognise the spirit which 
it breathes, and would judge the unfinished work 
of a deceased soldier with the al lowances which it 
justly claims. 
This hope has been amply confirmed by the 
result. Public opinion has spoken loudly in favour 

• Of Gneisenau.

EDITOR'S PREFACE. xxxiii

of the work, and from far and near the most 
indubitable proofs of its approval have not been 
wanting. 
That adverse voices indeed would be raised, that 
many a sentence, perhaps too harshly penned, 
would be controverted even with acrimony, was to 
be expected, and, while courtesy was observed, could 
neither offend nor surprise ; in some sense could 
only satisfy, for by such means the cause of truth 
is furthered, and truth was ever the first object of 
the Author. 
[Here follow some remarks which are omitted, as 
having reference to parts of previous publications 
unconnected with this volume. The Editor, after 
explaining that every thing has been published 
without alteration, proceeds : -] 
If it has thus happened that some too severe 
criticisms have been placed on record, we can 
assure the reader that the Author would have 
hastened to qualify them, if he could have con 
jectured that his work would have been given to 
the world in this unfinished shape. Those who 
knew his noble and gentle nature know how unwil 
lingly he would have inJured the feelings of any 
one, how assiduously he ever strived to avoid or 
to soften angry collisions, and particularly how 
strongly he desired to see relations of friendship 
b 

xx xiv EDITOR'S PREFACE.

and confidence cultivated between Prussia and Austria. His censure was never based upon a feeling of hostility ; and if al l his writings should ever be in the hands of the public, his impartiality wil l be fully acknowledged.

Berlin, Dec.17. 1834.
